The pre-push scan and `install-test.sh`'s config-key leg are what keep that true. ★ Rotation remains available and pointless while the fleet is disposable; it becomes owed the moment any fleet node stops being disposable. **Cross-ref:** ODC-080 below.

> **★ ODC-080 (raised 2026-08-24) — THE SPORK GENERATION WALKTHROUGH SECURES THE WIF AND SAYS NOTHING ABOUT THE WALLET THAT HOLDS IT.** `ONBOARDING.md` §2 was fixed in `8c2a53f` so `dumpprivkey` redirects into a `0600` file under `umask 077` instead of printing the WIF to a terminal — but the disposable datadir it created, `$HOME/.hemis-spork`, still contains a `wallet.dat` holding **the same private key**, and the walkthrough never says to destroy it, encrypt it, or move it off the host. Every step from `:122` to `:206` uses that datadir and the document ends without disposing of it. ★ The narrow fix (redirect the WIF) was applied to the value that travelled through the terminal, and the copy that never travelled — and is therefore easy not to think about — was left where it lay. Owed: a closing step in §2 that treats the datadir as key material. **Cross-ref:** ODC-079.

> **★★ KDD-103 (adopted 2026-08-24) — THE CLASS: *WHAT BREAKS WHEN THE OPERATOR SET OPENS*.** Registered as a CLASS rather than as three unrelated rows, because the value is in making the next instance findable, and three rows do not do that. ★ **The defining test is not "is this bad on testnet?" — it is: does this property depend on every operator being someone we chose?** If yes, it is a member, and it is structurally broken the moment registration is permissionless rather than merely inconvenient until then. ★ **Why the class exists at all:** several findings this week were softened with *"it's only testnet"*, and that reasoning does not hold for two independent reasons — this is the design five operators will learn and outsiders will inspect, and, more importantly, a closed operator set is not a mitigation, it is an ASSUMPTION that the product's own roadmap removes. A defect held shut by the guest list has no defence at all once the guest list is abolished. ★ **MEMBERS (each carries its own row; this entry owns the shape, not the details):** (1) **KDD-085 sign-over-P2P** — the fan-out authenticates with the DIALLING node's own `rpcuser`/`rpcpassword` (`ptx_fanout.cpp:612-616`), sent as HTTP Basic to all eleven members of every roll, and the caller cannot choose its quorum (selection is by tip hash). With permissionless registration that credential goes to *whoever registered*; there is no `-rpcwhitelist` in this fork (grep: 0 hits), so one hostile member gets the ENTIRE RPC surface — `stop`, `setban`, `invalidateblock`, the wallet — on every caller that ever routed to it. **Permissionless GMs and RPC fan-out are incompatible, and that is a property of the architecture, not of the firewall.** (2) **W4-b consensus verification of the beacon** — a verifiable randomness beacon whose chain does not verify its own randomness is a claim, not a proof; while the operator set is closed, "no staker would" is doing the work that consensus should do. ★ See its row for the correction that its SIGNATURE half already landed (`81fcf26`) and only the beacon/results DERIVATION remains — the class membership survives the correction, because the unverified half is exactly the half an outsider would check. ★ **The corollary that makes the class actionable:** for anything on the pre-mainnet list, ask *what is holding this shut today* before asking *how bad is it today*. If the answer is "who we let in", it is a member and its severity is the severity it will have, not the severity it has. ★ **Not a member, for contrast (the class must exclude something or it is not a class):** ODC-079 (the fleet's shared RPC credential is in the public repo) is bad now and no worse later — opening the operator set does not change it. **Cross-ref:** KDD-085, W4-b, BUG-052, ODC-081, KDD-102.

> **★ ODC-081 (raised 2026-08-24) — TWO SHIPPED ARTEFACTS STILL SAY W4-b IS UNBUILT, FOUR WEEKS AFTER ITS SIGNATURE HALF LANDED, AND THE CLAIM WAS BELIEVED.** `src/primitives/transaction.h:540-542` reads *"NOT a trustworthy trigger input until the consensus `quorum_sig` verify lands (W4-b): until then any staker can stamp an arbitrary quorum_hash"*, and `testnet/explorer/ptx_verify.py:3-11` repeats it with the supporting greps. **The verify landed in `81fcf26` (2026-07-27) at `evo/specialtx_validation.cpp:1090-1114`** — an ancestor of `HEAD` and of the released commit `b637751`, so it is in the binary operators are running. ★ **The greps that justified the stale claim are both TRUE and both irrelevant:** `PTX_MapBeacon` really is absent from `validation.cpp` and `quorum_sig` really does appear there zero times — because PTX special-transaction validation does not live in `validation.cpp`, it lives in `evo/specialtx_validation.cpp`. **A grep over the wrong file proves nothing and reads exactly like proof.** ★ The explorer's sentence is half-right and that is what made it durable: the beacon/results half is still accurate (`PTX_BLS_SigToBeacon` has exactly one non-test caller, `rpc/ptx.cpp:382`; `PTX_MapBeacon` count in `src/evo/` = 0), so anyone spot-checking the paragraph confirms the half that is true and carries the half that is not. ★ **CONSEQUENCE, and why this is a register row rather than a comment fix:** it became the premise of a costing task — *should we add a BLS pairing to consensus?* — whose answer was already "it has been there for four weeks, at 2.4 ms, on every node, and nobody costed it." A stale comment that understates what ships is worse than no comment: it suppresses the measurement of a cost already being paid. **FIX SHAPE:** correct both, and state the residual precisely — `beacon == SHA256(quorum_sig)` and `results == PTX_MapBeacon(beacon, …)` are unchecked; the signature and the quorum identity are not. **Cross-ref:** BUG-052, KDD-103, doc/ptx/W4B_COST_AND_KDD085_SCOPE.md.

> **★ ODC-082 (raised 2026-08-25) — A GREP OVER THE WRONG FILE IS A TRUE MEASUREMENT OF THE WRONG THING, AND IT READS AS PROOF.** Raised against the W4-b costing premise (ODC-081) but recorded as its own row because it is the operational form of "source before label" and it recurs. The premise was *"`quorum_sig` appears in `validation.cpp` zero times, so consensus does not verify it"* — the count is exactly right and the conclusion is false, because PTX special-tx validation lives in `evo/specialtx_validation.cpp`, not `validation.cpp`. ★ **This is the same shape as the `lastPaidHeight` misread and the backtick-scan false positives: an instrument run cleanly against the wrong subject.** A zero from `grep quorum_sig validation.cpp` is not evidence about what consensus checks unless you have first established that `validation.cpp` is where the check would be — and that step was skipped because the filename looked authoritative. ★ **The discipline, stated so it is checkable:** before concluding ABSENCE from a grep, establish that you searched WHERE THE THING WOULD BE — name the file the code WOULD live in and confirm the search covered it. A grep proves absence only over the ground it actually walked. **Cross-ref:** ODC-081, KDD-102 (a build config that skips a check ≈ a check that passes — this is its search-level twin), KDD-103.

> **★★ KDD-104 (adopted 2026-08-25) — A CHECKLIST OF PIN SITES IS ITSELF A PIN SITE, AND A CHECKLIST WRITTEN IN THE PAST TENSE IS NOT A CHECKLIST AT ALL.** Raised out of BUG-054, where the `v0.1.1-testnet` cut repinned `install.sh` and left the operator's literal FIRST command fetching a superseded build. ★ **The list existed and it named the file that broke.** `PTX_TESTNET_RELEASE.md:118-123` enumerated the pin sites, and `vps-install.sh:37` plus the `raw.githubusercontent` bootstrap URL are both *on it*. So the failure was not an unknown site — it was a known site on a list nobody re-ran. ★ **Why nobody re-ran it: it was written as a record, not as an instruction.** Every entry carries a literal `v0.1.0-testnet` and a `✓`, under the sentence *"all four are already flipped"*. That is a report of a completed sweep. Read at the next cut it says **the work is done**, because for the tag it names, it was. A checklist whose items are stamped with the answer from last time cannot ask you a question. ★ **And it was incomplete regardless** — six live pin sites exist and it listed five, omitting `ONBOARDING.md:282`'s value table, the one an operator is most likely to copy by hand. ★ **Generalisation, and it is the KDD-097/098/099 family again from a new angle:** those were cases where *a declaration promised something the mechanism did not deliver*. This is the case where **the declaration is a snapshot of a past state and is read as a statement about the present**. Ask of any list: *what makes this go stale, and what would tell me it had?* If the answer is "someone remembers", it is documentation, not a control. ★ **Acted on, not just recorded:** `testnet/operator/pin-check.sh` replaces the list with a check. Three properties make it not-a-list — (i) **the file set is discovered** via `git ls-files`, so a new file under `testnet/operator/` is covered the day it lands; (ii) **the expected tag is read from `install.sh:22`**, not typed, so there is no second place to update; (iii) **the historical exemptions are themselves verified** — an exemption that stops matching is reported as `STALE EXEMPTION`, so the allowlist cannot rot silently the way the checklist did. RED-proven on all three legs, including reintroducing BUG-054 verbatim.

> **★★ KDD-105 (adopted 2026-08-25) — A CREDENTIAL USED WHERE THE QUESTION IS NOT ABOUT IDENTITY IS A DESIGN ERROR, NOT A CONFIGURATION ONE; REDUCING ITS BLAST RADIUS DOES NOT FIX IT.** Raised when per-operator caller credentials were proposed as a mitigation for KDD-085's exposure, and rejected. ★ **The deciding test: ask what question the credential answers.** `gm_bls_sign` asks *"should I sign this?"*, and its actual authorisation is the BUG-032 payment gate — *"is there a funded commitment for this exact (round_seed, quorum_hash)?"* — which **every GM answers independently, from data it already holds, about a caller it has never heard of** (`rpc/ptx.cpp:550-628` never reads caller identity; `ptx_mempool.cpp:413-449` is the whole decision). The credential answers *"do you know a secret?"*: a **proxy the network cannot verify**, which every member must hold **identically**, and which any holder can replay against any other member. ★ **Why the mitigation is not a fix.** Issuing five credentials instead of one narrows blast radius and leaves the model intact — it still requires each GM to **enumerate the callers it trusts**, and in the real network eleven quorum members may be eleven different operators, none of whom knows who the caller is and none of whom should trust the others. **A mitigation that scales the damage without changing the question is a delay, not a remedy.** ★ **Generalisation:** when a secret is used to authorise, ask whether the thing being authorised is checkable from public state. If it is, the secret stands in for a check the system can already perform, and is **strictly weaker** than that check — it can be stolen, must be distributed, must be rotated, and forces mutual trust between parties who otherwise need none. ★ **The positive form, which is what threshold BLS exists for:** eleven mutually-distrusting strangers each reaching the same verdict from public data. ★ Same family as KDD-097/098/099 — *a mechanism asserting something it does not deliver* — but one level up: not a setting that misstates what it **binds**, but **a whole mechanism answering the wrong question, correctly.** **Cross-ref:** KDD-085 scope + plan in `doc/ptx/W4B_COST_AND_KDD085_SCOPE.md` §7 and §9; ODC-079, ODC-083.

> **★★ KDD-106 (adopted 2026-08-25) — A MEASUREMENT TAKEN ON THE FLEET IS NOT AUTOMATICALLY CONSERVATIVE, AND WHERE THE FLEET'S CONFIG DIFFERS FROM AN OPERATOR'S IT CAN BE WRONG IN THE REASSURING DIRECTION.** Found while quantifying KDD-085's DoS surface (`W4B_COST_AND_KDD085_SCOPE.md` §9.4). The cost of the `PTX_RollCommitmentPresent` mempool scan is **linear in mempool size**, so the number depends entirely on `maxmempool`. ★ **The fleet runs `maxmempool=50`; `testnet/operator/install.sh` never sets `maxmempool` at all, so every real operator gets the `DEFAULT_MAX_MEMPOOL_SIZE = 300` (`policy/policy.h:25`) — a factor of six.** A fleet measurement of this surface understates the operator's exposure 6×. ★ **Why this is the dangerous direction.** The instinct that "the fleet is 161 nodes on one box, so it is harsher than production" is true for *contention* and false for *anything read from a config the fleet sets differently*. A harsh-environment assumption makes a fleet number feel like an upper bound when it is a **lower** one. ★ **The rule:** before quoting a fleet measurement as a bound, **diff the fleet's effective config against what `install.sh` writes**, and state which side of the real value the number sits on. The fleet is authoritative for behaviour and only conditionally authoritative for magnitude. ★ Same family as ODC-082 (*a grep over the wrong file is a true measurement of the wrong thing*) and KDD-104 (*a checklist written in the past tense reads as proof*): **a true number attached to the wrong subject, which reads as evidence.** **Cross-ref:** KDD-085, ODC-082, KDD-104.

> **★ ODC-083 (raised 2026-08-25) — THE SHARED `ptxcaller` CREDENTIAL IS ACCEPTED ACROSS ptx001-003, WITH A WRITTEN EXPIRY.** Recorded as a **deliberate acceptance**, not a drift, because KDD-085 is not landing before the launch chain. **The acceptance, verbatim:**
>
> > **The shared `ptxcaller` credential does not leave Vileda's own hosts. KDD-085 lands before operator #2 is invited.**
>
> ★ **Why this sentence is written down rather than assumed — do not delete it as boilerplate:** *an acceptance without an expiry is how the fleet arrived at `rpcallowip=0.0.0.0/0` and ODC-079.* ODC-079 records the fleet's shared credential as *"accepted implicitly, not decided"*; this row exists so the same thing does not happen twice, in the operator-facing configuration, where it would matter. ★ **Why the acceptance is defensible here and per-operator credentials were not (KDD-105):** ptx001-003 are Vileda's own hosts, so **nothing is being assumed that is not true**. Per-operator credentials would have *pretended* to fix the model; this does not pretend — it accepts a bounded exposure while the model is fixed. ★ **The boundary, stated precisely:** the line is **the moment the credential reaches a host its holder did not choose.** Cloning onto ptx001-003 does not cross it; inviting operator #2 does. (This replaces the looser framing "the line is distribution".) ★ **Scope of the exposure while accepted:** the caller transmits the plaintext `ptxcaller` password to all eleven members of every roll as HTTP Basic (`ptx/ptx_fanout.cpp:612-616`); `rpcauth` protects the GM config at rest and nothing in transit; `rpcallowip=<caller>` is a real second factor, so the realistic severity is *"one hostile operator **plus** one widened config"*. **Discharged by:** KDD-085 landing, which deletes both lines from every GM config. **Cross-ref:** KDD-085, KDD-105, ODC-079, ODC-080.

> ★★ **ODC-083 AMENDMENT (2026-08-31) — DISCHARGED WITHOUT EVER BEING EXERCISED, AND THE DISTINCTION IS THE POINT.** Vileda's schedule decision: **KDD-085 lands before the chain starts**, not after, and the clones to ptx001-003 wait for it. So the shared `ptxcaller` credential **never reaches a second host at all** — the acceptance above is retired by the *exposure ceasing to exist*, not by its trigger firing at the boundary it named. ★ **Why this is recorded rather than quietly deleted, and why it is the GOOD outcome:** an acceptance normally ends one of two ways — its expiry fires and the work is done under time pressure, or the expiry is forgotten and the acceptance becomes permanent (which is exactly how the fleet arrived at `rpcallowip=0.0.0.0/0`, ODC-079). **This is a third and better ending: the schedule moved so the bounded exposure was never taken on.** ★ **The transferable part:** when an acceptance is written with a real trigger, *"move the work earlier than the trigger"* becomes a visible option — you can see what the acceptance is buying and decide it is not worth buying. An acceptance with no expiry never offers that choice, because nobody can see what it costs. **So the value of writing the expiry down was realised even though the expiry never fired.** ★ Status: **ODC-083 CLOSED — never exercised.** The two config lines are still present in `install.sh:865-890` and are deleted by KDD-085 component 4; until then they are unshipped rather than accepted.

> **★★ ODC-084 (raised 2026-08-25) — SYBIL / POOL-SHARE ACCUMULATION IS NOT REGISTERED ANYWHERE, AND WAS NOT UNTIL THIS ROW.** Found by checking KDD-085's honest-boundary table against the register rather than assuming: **`grep -i sybil` over every document in this repository returns zero hits.** ★ **The gap:** anyone may run a gamemaster, so anyone may run many. Selection by tip hash closes quorum-*shopping* (BUG-033 — an attacker cannot choose **which** quorum they are in) but does nothing about **pool share**: an actor holding a fraction *f* of registered GMs receives approximately *f* of the seats in every quorum, and at `t = formed_size/2 + 1` = **6 of 11** needs only a majority of one quorum to produce a signature. The barrier is collateral, and on ptxtestnet collateral is **100 HMS** (`chainparams.cpp:757`), not 1000. ★ **Interaction with ODC-036/KDD-048:** the `t = n/2+1` stopgap is what makes six enough; the pre-documented `t=7-at-n=11` upgrade raises the coalition requirement, so these two items should be reasoned about together rather than separately. ★ **Why it was missed, which is the transferable part:** *"permissionless"* was discussed throughout as an **operational** property — who may join, who must be trusted, what an operator must configure — and never as an **economic** one: *how much does controlling six seats cost?* A threat model that enumerates capabilities without pricing them will miss this class every time. **Cross-ref:** KDD-077 (threat model — §3's malice-vs-error limit is the nearest existing coverage and does not reach this), ODC-036, KDD-048, KDD-085 §9.12. **Not a blocker for testnet; required before any value rides the beacon.**

> **★★ KDD-107 (adopted 2026-09-01) — AN EMPIRICAL VALIDATION IS BOUND TO THE CONFIGURATION IT WAS RUN ON; REPLACING THE SUBSTRATE SILENTLY INVALIDATES IT, AND A "COMPLETED" ROW CANNOT KNOW THAT.** Found by the gap sweep, asking of each phase-plan exit criterion *"does testnet exercise this, or does it look like it does?"* ★ **The instance:** `PTX_ROADMAP.md` Completed #1 is *"Phase 0 — randomness validation (NIST suite, final pass)"*. Completed #18 repointed the beacon from the trusted dealer to DKG output, and #19 (KDD-069) **retired the dealer entirely**. So the only statistical validation this project has of its beacon was performed on **an RNG configuration that no longer exists** — different key origin, different `n`, different `t`, different member selection. Nothing re-ran it, and nothing in the Completed list records that #18/#19 invalidated #1. ★ **Why a status list cannot catch this by itself:** rows are ordered by *when work finished*, and a later row silently changing an earlier row's premise leaves no mark — #1 and #18 are both true statements, ten rows apart, neither citing the other. **BUG-053's shape, applied to a plan instead of a document.** ★ **The rule:** every empirical or statistical result gets its **substrate recorded with it** — what was measured, on what configuration — and any change to that configuration re-opens the result. A validation without a recorded substrate is a claim, not a measurement. ★ Same family as KDD-106 (*a true number attached to the wrong subject*) and KDD-104 (*a checklist in the past tense reads as proof*), and one level up from both: **not a number attached to the wrong subject, but a correct result whose subject was later replaced underneath it.** **Cross-ref:** ODC-085, KDD-069, KDD-104, KDD-106, PTX_ROADMAP Completed #1/#18/#19.

> **★★ ODC-085 (raised 2026-09-01) — THE BEACON'S STATISTICAL VALIDATION IS BOUND TO A RETIRED SUBSTRATE, AND NOTHING RE-RUNS IT.** The concrete instance of KDD-107, registered separately because it needs doing, not only knowing. ★ **State, source-checked:** `grep -riE "nist|chi-squared|χ²|fisher-yates|uniformity"` over the whole repository returns **one** roadmap line (Completed #1) and **one** test-suite design row — `W25B_FLEET_TEST_SUITE.md` D1, *"distribution evenness over a large sample (≥500 rolls, χ²-style against uniform over 8)"*, tagged **FLEET-NEW** in a document whose own first line reads *"DESIGN only — nothing here is built or run."* There is **no evidence artifact anywhere** for the Phase 0 pass: no results file, no log, no doc. ★ **Why this matters more here than for a typical exit criterion:** the deliverable is a **verifiable randomness beacon**. Output uniformity is not a nice-to-have property of it — it is the product. And the enforcement gap already registered (2c-iii / ODC-081: `beacon == SHA256(quorum_sig)` and `results == PTX_MapBeacon(...)` are not consensus-checked) means the chain does not check the mapping either, so **neither the statistics nor the consensus rules currently constrain the output**, from different directions. ★ **What is NOT claimed:** there is no evidence the output is non-uniform. Threshold BLS signatures are deterministic and uniform by construction, and the repoint changed the key origin rather than the signature scheme, so the prior is strongly that this passes. **The defect is that nobody can point at a run** — and "it should be fine" is the reasoning this register exists to refuse. ★ **Minimum to close:** re-run a distribution test against DKG-sourced beacon output at testnet's actual shape (n=11 drawn from a 20-GM pool, `t = n/2+1`), and record the substrate with the result per KDD-107. W25B D1 is the design; it needs building and running. **Trigger: before any value rides the beacon** — the same bar as ODC-084, and they should be scheduled together. **Cross-ref:** KDD-107, ODC-081, ODC-084, KDD-069, W25B_FLEET_TEST_SUITE.md D1.

> **★ ODC-086 (raised 2026-09-01) — THE FAULT CLASSES TOXIPROXY USED TO INJECT ARE NOW OBSERVED ONLY IF THEY HAPPEN TO OCCUR, AND NOTHING ON THE OPERATOR TESTNET IS WATCHING.** The phase plan retires deliberate fault injection after Phase 2 on the reasoning that *"the real network provides fault conditions from Phase 3."* That reasoning is sound and the substitution was deliberate (`testnet/w2fleet/ops/netem_mesh.sh:9` records choosing netem over Toxiproxy, with the fan-out addressing rationale). ★ **The gap is the second half nobody wrote:** a real network *supplies* fault conditions, it does not *report* them. Every fault-observation instrument in the tree — `latency_battery.py`, `latency_ladder.sh`, `ceremony_sweep.py`, `bug034_detector.py`, `validate_fleet.py` — lives under `testnet/w2fleet/` and is pointed at the **docker fleet**; `testnet/harness/` and `testnet/scenarios/` carry no partition, latency or reorg-during-round detector. So on the operator testnet, partition / latency / reorg-during-round become **incidental rather than instrumented**: they will happen, and the first anyone learns of one is a roll that failed for an unexplained reason. ★ **Why that is worse than the fleet case it replaces:** on the fleet these classes were injected, so their signature was known and expected. On testnet they arrive unannounced, on someone else's hardware, and the diagnosis starts from zero — which is the cost pattern ODC-055 and the caller1 banlist episode already charged this project once each. ★ **Minimum to close:** decide, explicitly, which fault classes are worth an always-on detector on the operator testnet versus which are accepted as diagnose-on-report, and record that decision. **Not a build item until that decision exists.** **Trigger: before operator #2 is invited** — the same boundary as ODC-083, because it is the point at which a fault occurs on hardware nobody here can inspect. **Cross-ref:** ODC-055, KDD-077 §5, W25B_FLEET_TEST_SUITE.md.

> **★ ODC-087 (raised 2026-09-01) — `inherited_features.py` REPORTS A REWARD DIVERGENCE THAT WAS FIXED, ON EVERY RUN, NAMING A CONSTANT THE CHAIN NO LONGER USES.** `testnet/scenarios/inherited_features.py:585` compares the calibrated GM coinbase against `round(6 * COIN)` and, on mismatch, prints *"diverges from nNewGMBlockReward (600000000 sat = 6 HMS)"*; the surrounding prose (`:30`, `:41`) states *"nNewGMBlockReward (6 HMS from CPTXBeaTestNetParams)"*. ★ **Both are now false.** `2c9b26d` changed the value, and `chainparams.cpp` sets `nNewGMBlockReward = 2.674999995 * COIN` on **both** `CPTXBeaTestNetParams` (`:1062`) and `CPTXTestNetParams` (`:794`); 6 HMS survives only on regtest (`:588`). The effective on-chain payout the scenario measures — ~2.675 HMS — now **matches** the schedule constant, so the divergence the observation was written to flag **no longer exists**. ★ **The failure mode is not a red test** (the assertion is calibrate-then-assert, which is the right design and is unaffected) **— it is a permanently-firing OBSERVATION that misstates chainparams**, on a run whose whole purpose is to characterise inherited reward behaviour for an audience that has not read chainparams. The first operator-testnet run of this scenario will report a resolved defect as live. ★ Same family as KDD-097/098/099 — *a mechanism asserting something it does not deliver* — and the mechanism here is a diagnostic, which is the worst place for it: **a broken instrument reads as a finding.** **Minimum to close:** compare against `nNewGMBlockReward` read from the chain rather than a literal, or delete the branch. Small, and it should not ride a larger change. **Cross-ref:** `2c9b26d`, INHERITED_PARAMS_AUDIT.md, KDD-097/098/099.

> **★ ODC-088 (raised 2026-09-01) — THE OPERATOR GUIDE DOCUMENTS `protx_update_service` ONLY FOR PoSe RECOVERY, AND ITS RECIPE IS WRONG FOR THE VOLUNTARY CASE.** `testnet/operator/OPERATOR_GUIDE.md:547` is the sole occurrence, inside the PoSe-recovery section, and it instructs passing the BLS secret as argument 4 — correctly, and with a good explanation: a **banned** gamemaster cannot supply its own key because `GetValidGM` returns `nullptr` for it (`deterministicgms.cpp:114-121`). ★ **But an operator who simply moves house has not been banned.** `protx_update_service` takes 2 to 4 arguments (`rpc/rpcevo.cpp:916`); for a healthy gamemaster the key argument is unnecessary, and the documented recipe therefore tells an operator to handle their BLS secret in the one situation where the guide's own rule — *"the BLS secret never moves"*, of which the banned case is explicitly called **the single exception** — did not need breaking. ★ **The realistic trigger is mundane and certain:** a new VPS, an ISP change, a home connection renumbering. Five independent operators over a testnet's lifetime will hit this, and the guide currently routes them through the recovery procedure, which also implies their node is broken when it is not. ★ **Minimum to close:** a short "changing your gamemaster's address deliberately" subsection — two arguments, no BLS secret, run from the wallet host — placed **outside** the PoSe section, with the existing recovery recipe cross-referencing it for the difference. **Trigger: before operator #2 is invited.** **Cross-ref:** ODC-083, KDD-085 §9.6 (which deletes other operator failure modes but not this one).

> **★ ODC-089 (raised 2026-09-01) — PHASE 4 MULTIPLAYER SESSIONS HAVE NO ROW ANYWHERE; THE SCOPE EXISTS ONLY IN CONVERSATION.** `grep -ri "multiplayer\|multi-player"` over **every markdown file in the repository returns zero hits** — the same instrument that found ODC-084, run on a different word. The finding that multiplayer *"will require another major update"* was made in session and never landed anywhere portable, so it is currently indistinguishable from forgotten. ★ **Why this is a register defect rather than a scoping one:** the project's roadmap file exists precisely because *"the program plan must survive a clone"* (its own opening line, the KDD-065-class fix). A phase-4 scope item that lives only in a chat transcript is the exact failure that file was created to prevent, reproduced one phase further out. ★ **Not a blocker for testnet, and not an argument for scoping it now.** The minimum is a single line in `PTX_ROADMAP.md` recording that Phase 4 multiplayer is **unscoped and known to need a major update**, so the next person to plan against it discovers that from the repository rather than from having been in the room. **Cross-ref:** KDD-065, PTX_ROADMAP.md.

> **★ ODC-090 (raised 2026-09-01) — A BAD PARTIAL SIGNATURE FAILS THE ROUND WITH NO ATTRIBUTION, AND KDD-085 DOES NOT CHANGE THAT.** Surfaced while building the caller side (KDD-085 component 3), registered because the new `signer_protx` field could easily be *read* as fixing it. ★ **State, source-checked:** the caller collects raw 96-byte partials, maps `node_id -> share_index` from its own `PTXDKGSigningCtx`, recovers from the first `t`, and verifies **only the recovered signature** against `group_pk` (`rpc/ptx.cpp:340-379`). No partial is ever verified individually. So one member returning a well-formed but wrong partial produces a recovered signature that fails `PTX_BLS_Verify`, and the round fails **naming nobody** — the caller cannot tell which member did it, or whether it was malice or a corrupted share. ★ **Why it is not fixable in component 3:** per-partial verification needs each member's public share `pk_i`, derivable from the quorum's vvec. `CPTXQuorumRecord` persists `group_pk_bytes` and **`vvec_hash`** (`ptx_quorum_store.h:137-138`) — the hash, not the vvec. So `pk_i` is not reconstructible from stored state, and the check cannot be written without a persistence change. ★ **What `signer_protx` DOES and does not do** (the distinction this row exists to protect): it lets the caller reject a reply whose claimed signer disagrees with the member it asked, catching **misrouting and mislabelling**. It says nothing about whether the *signature* is correct. Claiming otherwise would be exactly the KDD-105 error — *a mechanism asserting something it does not deliver* — one file away from where KDD-105 was adopted. ★ **Consequences if left:** a single faulty or hostile member can fail every round it is selected into, indefinitely, and the operator-facing symptom is *"rolls fail sometimes"* with no member named. PoSe cannot score it either, because PoSe scores participation and this member **participates**. ★ **Minimum to close:** persist the vvec (or the per-member `pk_i` vector) in the quorum record, verify each partial on receipt, and drop-and-name a member that fails. **Trigger: before any value rides the beacon** — it joins ODC-084 and ODC-085 at that bar; not a testnet blocker, since the failure is loud and safe (a round fails, nothing wrong is produced). **Cross-ref:** KDD-105, KDD-052, ODC-085, ODC-084, KDD-085 §9.13(f).

> **★ ODC-091 (raised 2026-09-01) — A STALE COMMENT ON A LIVE CONSENSUS BOUND IS A DEFECT, NOT UNTIDINESS, AND THIS IS THE SECOND ONE THIS WEEK.** `specialtx_validation.cpp`'s ODC-073 anchor-lag check carried *"Window 0 == disabled (main/test/ptxtest); ptxbea/regtest set 60"*. The `ptxtest` half was false: `nPTXSeedHeightWindow = 60` on ptxtestnet (`chainparams.cpp:1011`) as well as ptxbea and regtest; only main/test leave it at 0. Fixed in `1b112bd`, comment only — the check reads chainparams and was always correct. ★ **Why it earns a register row rather than just a fix:** the comment described a **live consensus bound as absent**, on the network about to launch, which is wrong in the direction that invites action. A reader checking whether the stale-seed rule applies to testnet concludes it does not; a reader tidying up concludes the `ptxtest` entry is the truth and the chainparams value is the mistake, and *"fixes"* it back. **A stale comment next to a consensus rule is a trap for the next person, not a cosmetic issue.** ★ **The pattern, which is the transferable part:** this is the third instance this week of a statement true when written and false since, with nothing flagging the transition — `bfea163`'s *"UNCOMPILED"* (§9.13(b)), the roadmap's Phase 0 randomness row (KDD-107/ODC-085), and now this. All three were found by *reading the claim and then checking it*, never by the claim announcing itself. ★ **The rule this adds to KDD-107:** where a comment states which **networks** a consensus rule is active on, it is restating chainparams and will drift. Prefer naming the parameter and letting the reader look, or cite the chainparams lines — as the corrected comment now does. **Cross-ref:** KDD-107, KDD-104, ODC-073, `1b112bd`.

> **★★ ODC-092 (raised 2026-09-01, AMENDED THE SAME DAY) — THE RELEASE BINARY CANNOT PRINT ITS OWN RELEASE NAME, AND THE FIRST DIAGNOSIS WAS ONLY HALF OF IT.** Operators are told *"verify you are on `v0.1.2-testnet`"*; `Hemisd -version` prints `v1.3.1.0-9ff584a` and there is no way to get the tag name out of it. Two separate investigations this week were spent reconciling a version string against a tag name because of this. ★ **First cause (found by reading `genbuild.sh`):** it overrides the numeric client version with the tag name, but via `git describe --abbrev=0` **without `--tags`** — annotated tags only. Every PTX release tag is **lightweight** (`git cat-file -t v0.1.2-testnet` → `commit`), so `describe` falls through to the nearest annotated tag (`first-quorum`), the HEAD comparison fails, and the numeric fallback is used. ★★ **SECOND CAUSE, AND THE FIRST FIX WOULD NOT HAVE WORKED WITHOUT IT:** the release workflow **builds at `build-and-release.yml:401-446` and only creates the tag at `:490`**. At build time no tag exists at HEAD, so genbuild cannot see one *however it is created*. **Making tags annotated is necessary and not sufficient; the tag must also exist before the build.** The fix is to create and push the annotated tag first and dispatch the workflow from that ref. ★ **Why the amendment matters more than the original row:** the first diagnosis was reached by reading `genbuild.sh` and reasoning about it, and it produced a recommendation that would have been dispatched, believed, and *still not printed the tag name* — the failure would then have looked like the fix not working rather than the fix being incomplete. It was caught only because the next instruction was **"confirm by building, not by reading"**. ★ **Verified empirically (px1, 2026-09-01):** annotated tag at HEAD + clean tree → `#define BUILD_DESC "<tag>"` in `src/obj/build.h`, and `-version` prints the tag name. ★ **Known trade:** `BUILD_DESC` *replaces* the version string rather than appending, so a tagged build carries the tag and **no commit hash** — better for *"am I on the release?"*, worse for identifying a stale binary, which is what the commit hash served during the `4d558e6` retraction. ★ **Durability, which is the point of the row:** the requirement is written into `PTX_TESTNET_RELEASE.md` §3.0a and **checked** by `testnet/operator/pin-check.sh`, which now fails on a lightweight expected tag and states the ordering rule when the tag does not exist yet. ★ Third instance this week of *a verification instruction naming something the tool cannot report* (ODC-091, KDD-107) — and the first one where a **check** now exists to catch the fourth. **Cross-ref:** KDD-107, ODC-091, BUG-054, KDD-104.

> **★★ KDD-108 (adopted 2026-09-01) — A REWRITE CAN DROP A GUARANTEE WITH NO TEXTUAL FOOTPRINT IN THE DIFF, AND REVIEW CANNOT SEE AN ABSENCE.** Found while auditing KDD-085's sign-round state machine, and registered **apart from** the `UNSENT`/`INFLIGHT` no-exit pair it was found beside, because the generalisation is different and worse. ★ **The instance:** the RPC fan-out bounded a member that answers *"commitment not seen"* forever — `FANOUT_MAX_ATTEMPTS`, derived as `FANOUT_WALL_MS / FANOUT_RETRY_MS`, a retry counter in the dialer's own loop. KDD-085 component 3 replaced the dialer with a P2P request/reply state machine, and the cap did not come across: `RETRYABLE` exited only back to `INFLIGHT`, uncapped, so such a member cycled until the wall and never became absorbing. **The P2P caller was therefore STRICTLY WEAKER than the path it replaced, in exactly the case that path existed to handle.** ★★ **Why this is not just "a bug in the new code":** the bound lived in a *variable belonging to the deleted machinery*. When `ptx_fanout.cpp` was deleted, `FANOUT_MAX_ATTEMPTS` went with it — correctly, since nothing in the new design has dials or attempts. **There was no line left over to look wrong.** A reviewer reading the deletion sees a retry counter for a retry loop that no longer exists, and is right to see nothing. **The lost property had no textual footprint, so no amount of diff-reading could have caught it.** ★ **What DID catch it:** enumerating every state's transitions in and out and asking of each *"what exits this, and does that exit depend on the far side cooperating?"* — a property audit of the new mechanism, not a comparison of the two texts. It was found by inspection **after** the fleet had already found a sibling defect, which is the honest sequence: the first one cost a fleet run, the second and third did not. ★★ **THE RULE: when replacing a mechanism, enumerate what the OLD one GUARANTEED before confirming the new one guarantees the same things. A clean diff proves nothing about capabilities that had no textual footprint.** Write the old guarantees down as a list *before* deleting, and check the list against the replacement — the deletion commit is the last moment anyone can still read them. ★ Same family as ODC-081 and KDD-104 — *a true reading of the wrong subject* — but inverted: here the subject is **missing**, and the reading is of something that is no longer there to be read. Nearest existing cousin is BUG-036 REGISTER 2 (derive-don't-store: a lost write is invisible until it diverges); this is a lost *guarantee*, invisible until it fails to hold. ★ **Applied fix, for the record:** three separate bounds were **not** bolted on. A member gets one budget (`PTX_SIGNREQ_MEMBER_MS`, strictly inside the wall) and anything still non-absorbing when it expires retires — plus a `_COUNT` sentinel, a `static_assert` and a default-less switch, so a state added without being classified **fails to compile**. The guard was **proven to fire** by adding an unclassified state and watching the build reject it, not assumed from its silence. **Cross-ref:** KDD-085 §9.13, ODC-081, KDD-104, BUG-036, `fdee463`, `afcc897`.

> **★★ ODC-093 (raised 2026-09-01) — `nSupportedQuorums` ENFORCES NOTHING AT RUNTIME, WHILE ITS NAME AND HEADER COMMENT DESCRIBE A CEILING THAT DOES NOT EXIST.** ★ **What it actually does, which is the half the register was missing:** it is a **startup capacity assertion**, read only by `PTX_Formation_CheckParams` (`ptx_formation.cpp:110-158`) — reject if `<= 0`; **Guard 1 hard tier** (`:118`) reject if `nRotationInterval/nBoundaryInterval < L`, i.e. the rotation cadence cannot keep `L` quorums alive past `nRotationInterval`; ODC-054 coupling (`:140`) reject `L > 1` with `nReformGrace <= 0`; and an advisory thin-margin warning (`:152`). It declares *how many quorums this configuration is expected to sustain* and validates the cadence against that declaration. **It is never consulted when a quorum forms.** ★ **What it does NOT do:** cap the ACTIVE set. Measured on ptxbea, configured `L=8`: **13 simultaneously ACTIVE**. The real bound is **pool exhaustion** — the KDD-040 filter removes every member of every active quorum, formation needs `>= 11` eligible, so the active set grows to `floor(N_registered / 11)` and then deterministically skips. Measured exactly: 13 active, 143 of 153 members excluded, **pool = 10 against a floor of 11**, and `floor(153/11) = 13`. The fleet was sitting on its own ceiling. ★★ **Why this is registered even though the BEHAVIOUR IS CORRECT AND SETTLED.** The reform/retire equilibrium and the multi-quorum posture are designed, tested and previously argued; nothing about them is in question. The defect is purely that **a parameter named "supported quorums", plus a header comment describing a ceiling, read as authoritative for something the code does not implement** — and three turns of fleet-sizing analysis were built on that reading before a direct observation broke it. ★ Same shape as the KDD-066 `SELECTION RULE` correction on the same day: **a name or heading that asserts a constraint the code does not enforce is indistinguishable, to a reader, from one that does.** The generalisation there was about *ordering within a comment block*; this one is about *naming*. A parameter whose name states a bound should either enforce it or say in one line that it does not. ★ **Corollary, recorded so the arithmetic is not re-derived wrongly:** `W25B_FLEET_CONFIG.md`'s 98-GM / 8-quorum recipe is now **explained** — `floor(98/11) = 8`. The recipe's author was sizing to the exhaustion ceiling, so **the recipe is sound and re-runnable whenever the substrate exists**; it is not a number nobody can justify. **Cross-ref:** KDD-066 row (same-day, same shape), KDD-040, ODC-054, KDD-079, `W25B_FLEET_CONFIG.md`.

> **★★ ODC-094 (raised 2026-09-01, ACTED ON 2026-09-02) — RETIREMENT HAS NO FLOOR: THE LAST ACTIVE QUORUM CAN GO, AND THE OPERATOR GUIDE WAS PRESCRIBING THE EXACT CONFIGURATION WHERE THAT IS TOTAL OUTAGE.** ★ **The asymmetry, from source.** *Rotation* is atomic: `ptx_quorum_store.cpp:262-272` writes the successor record ACTIVE and only then calls `MarkSuperseded(predecessor)`, both inside one `CurTransaction` — the in-source comment "Record flip is block-atomic via CurTransaction" is accurate, and there is no block at which neither is ACTIVE. *Retirement/idle-reform* is not: nothing on that path consults how many quorums remain. The check is not missing-and-grepped-for — there is no count of the active set anywhere in the retire path to compare against a floor, which is why a keyword grep proves nothing here and the **condition** is what had to be shown. ★ **Reachability is narrow and should not be overstated:** the window opens only via idle or forced retirement, i.e. on an already-idle network, and a reform starts immediately. It is a liveness gap, not a safety one, and no signature is at risk in it. ★★ **What makes it worth a row is the interaction with ODC-093's real ceiling.** The active-quorum count is `floor(N_registered / 11)`. So: **N < 22 → L = 1 → the retirement window is TOTAL outage**, no second quorum exists to carry it; **N = 22 → drops to one** for the window; **N ≥ 33 → real redundancy.** ★★ **And the operator guide prescribed N = 20.** "5 operators × 4 GMs = 20" was written into `OPERATOR_GUIDE.md`, `GM_QUICKSTART.md`, `ONBOARDING.md`, `vps-install.sh` and `install.sh`'s completion output — landing every literal reader in the `L = 1` case, with `floor(20/11) = 1`. **ACTION TAKEN 2026-09-02: the per-operator count is out of the operator-facing documents entirely.** They now state a *shape* — one wallet machine; one gamemaster per host; `100 HMS` collateral **per gamemaster**; funding `(N × 100) + 100 HMS` — and say **N is agreed with the coordinator**. `GENESIS_BOOTSTRAP.md` is the single place a number is now chosen, per operator, and it carries the instruction to **sum the assigned N and check the network total against 11 before sending any coins**. `PTX_TESTNET_GENESIS_CONFIG.md` §0.1 gained a second superseding banner rather than an edit to the record. ★ **The generalisation, which is why this is not just a doc fix:** *a resilience requirement that is a property of the network total must not be expressed as a per-operator constant.* Five operators at 4 and four operators at 5 are the same network; only one of those numbers survives an operator joining or leaving, and the constant silently stops meaning what it was chosen to mean the moment the operator set changes — which is exactly KDD-103's class. ★ **The coordinator runs the documented configuration, not a privileged variant.** The three coordinator nodes install from the same `install.sh`, on the same ports, with the same loopback-only RPC, and take their GM count the same way; there is no coordinator-only path through the guide, and nothing in it assumes one. ★ **AMENDMENT 2026-09-03 (append-only; the record above stands as written):** the funding figure quoted above has changed from `(N × 100) + 100 HMS` to **`(N × 100) + 500 HMS`**, across all six live sites. **The reason is not fees — it is that operators are expected to STAKE.** Without them the coordinator is the only block producer and the network's liveness rests on three hosts, which is a concentration this row's own argument about `L = 1` should have made obvious. ★ The `100 HMS` collateral *per gamemaster* and the shape-not-a-number rule are unchanged. ★ **The margin's stated PURPOSE changed with it**, deliberately: the old text described the extra as covering "fees, a re-send and a mistyped address", which is true of 100 and would leave an operator reasonably wondering why they were sent 500 and leaving it idle. **A number whose reason is not stated gets used for the reason the reader guesses.** ★★ **OBSERVED LIVE 2026-09-01, and it landed exactly as predicted.** On the 13-GM fleet the sole ACTIVE quorum (`5d039377…`, formed h240) went `MarkReformed: quorum REFORMED at height 480` — and `ptx_quorum_list` at h481 returned **an empty array on every node**, caller included. A `ptx_roll` issued into that window failed with `PTX: no ACTIVE quorum for height 481 — cannot sign`. That is the `L = 1` outage this row describes, reproduced without being sought: the reform was on the idle path, on an idle network, and the window opened between two consecutive status queries a minute apart. ★ It is a **clean liveness failure** — an explicit refusal naming the cause, not a hang and not a wrong signature — which is the fail-safe behaviour KDD-077 ratified, and it is why this stays a liveness row rather than a safety one. The window closes when the follow-on ceremony completes. ★ **The prediction that mattered was the conditional one:** `floor(13/11) = 1`, so there was no second quorum to carry it, exactly as the N < 22 case says. ★ **Not fixed and deliberately so:** the retire path still has no floor. A floor is a consensus-visible rule about when a quorum may leave the active set and belongs with the reform-cadence work, not in a documentation pass — the doc change removes the *prescription* of the fragile case, it does not remove the case.

> **★★ ODC-095 (raised 2026-09-01) — `REFORMED` HAS NO NAME IN EITHER RPC THAT RENDERS IT: ONE SAYS `unknown(4)`, THE OTHER SAYS `state(4)`, AND THE LOG SAYS `REFORMED`. THE SWITCH THAT SHOULD HAVE CAUGHT IT HAS A `default:`.** ★ **Found by observation, not by review.** Watching the h480 reform on the 13-GM fleet (see ODC-094): `ptx_quorum_info` reported `"state": "unknown(4)"`, `ptx_quorum_list 479` reported `"state": "state(4)"`, and `debug.log` reported `MarkReformed: quorum REFORMED at height 480`. Three renderings of one value, and the only correct one is the one an operator is least likely to read. ★ **`unknown(4)` is not merely unhelpful, it is false.** `REFORMED = 4` (`ptx_quorum_store.h:74`) is a *designed, documented, blameless terminal state* with eleven lines of header comment distinguishing it from `DISBANDED`, and a live producer (`MarkReformed`, W4-f, drill-proven). The RPC tells an operator the node does not recognise a state the node itself just wrote and logged — which points diagnosis at store corruption or a version mismatch, neither of which is happening. ★★ **THE MECHANISM, which is the reusable part.** `rpc/ptx.cpp:1099-1104` switches over `PTXQuorumState` **with a `default:` arm**, so adding `REFORMED` to the enum compiled silently; `rpc/ptx.cpp:1169-1170` does not switch at all, it ternaries `ACTIVE` against a `strprintf` fallback, so it could never have been caught by any enum discipline. **This repository already contains the pattern that would have caught it** — KDD-085's `PTXMemberSignState` pairs a `_COUNT` sentinel, a `static_assert` on it, and a **default-less** switch, and that combination hard-failed the build when `TOO_OLD` was added until it was classified. The two mechanisms sit in the same tree, one enum apart, and only one of them turns a new enumerator into a compile error. ★ **The generalisation: a `default:` arm on a switch over a closed, project-owned enum converts a compile-time obligation into a runtime string.** It is not defensive — there is no untrusted input here, the value comes from a record this code wrote — it is the deletion of the only signal that would have fired. And a *second* renderer of the same enum that does not switch at all means the discipline has to be applied per-site, which is the argument for **one `PTXQuorumStateName()` used by both**, not for two corrected switches. ★★ **CORRECTION, MEASURED — the guard is the `static_assert`, NOT the missing `default:`, and the first framing of this row had it backwards.** The proposed durable fix was "remove the `default:` rather than add the missing case", on the assumption that a default-less switch with an unhandled enumerator fails the build. **It does not, in this build.** `-Werror` is opt-in (`configure.ac:348`, `--enable-werror`) and even when set adds only `-Werror=vla` and `-Werror=thread-safety-analysis`; `-Wswitch` is a plain warning. Measured on this toolchain with the compiler's own exit code isolated (not a pipeline's): a default-less switch missing an enumerator → **g++ exit 0**, `warning: enumeration value not handled in switch [-Wswitch]`; a `_COUNT` sentinel moved by an addition → **g++ exit 1**, `error: static assertion failed`. So what actually fired on `TOO_OLD` was the assert. ★ **The pair is the guard and neither half is sufficient:** the assert catches a state APPENDED; the default-less switch catches one inserted in the MIDDLE, which leaves `_COUNT` unchanged. The source comment at `ptx_sign_client.cpp:40` said exactly this and was read past. ★ **Why this correction is the load-bearing part of the row:** "remove the `default:`" is a plausible, satisfying, wrong-by-half fix that would have bought a warning nobody reads in a 500-file build, while feeling like it had closed the hole. Anyone repeating the reasoning without the measurement repeats the mistake. See ODC-096 for the sweep this correction reframed. ★ **Scope note:** cosmetic in consequence, structural in cause, and it lands on the operator-facing surface during the exact window ODC-094 is about — the one time someone is reading `ptx_quorum_info` under pressure. Fix is one helper plus removing the `default:`; the `static_assert`/`_COUNT` treatment should follow the KDD-085 shape.

> **★★ ODC-096 (raised 2026-09-02) — ENUM-GROWTH GUARDS ARE PRESENT WHERE SOMEONE HAPPENED TO BUILD ONE AND ABSENT EVERYWHERE ELSE: ONE OF TEN PTX ENUMS IS COMPILE-ENFORCED, THE OTHER NINE REST ON WARNINGS NOBODY READS.** Swept after ODC-095 by brace-matching every `switch` in any file referencing a PTX-owned enum and classifying each by the only test that matters — ***would a new enumerator compile*, not *are today's cases complete***. Result: **10 switches over 10 enums; 6 carry a `default:`.** But that split is not the finding: per ODC-095's measured correction, the four *without* a `default:` are also unguarded, because `-Wswitch` only warns here. **`PTXMemberSignState` (KDD-085) is the sole enum in the tree with a `_COUNT` + `static_assert` tripwire, and the sole one ever observed to fire.** The first pass of this sweep called the other four "guarded by exhaustiveness" — that label was wrong and is corrected here. ★★ **RANKED BY CONSEQUENCE, and the top entry is not in the human-readable class the sweep was launched to find.** **(1) `ptx_quorum_store.cpp:55`, `PTX_QuorumRecordActiveAt` — a CONSENSUS predicate.** `default: return false`. This is the KDD-072 P-b4 as-of check that every consumer reads, validator V12 included, and that P-b4 documented as the place where `>=` versus `>` is a chain split. A future state left to the default is silently classified **not-active**: the quorum vanishes from the active set, and nodes on mixed code then disagree about which quorums are active. `return false` is a defensible fail-safe; the defect is that the classification is made **silently**, in a consensus predicate, by an arm nobody revisits. Nothing about the operator-facing switches would have led anyone here. **(2) `rpc/ptx.cpp:1098` and `:1169`** — ODC-095 proper; note `:1169` was a **ternary, not a switch**, so no enum discipline of any strength could have caught it, which is the argument for **one shared renderer** over N corrected switches. **(3) `ptx_ceremony_driver.cpp:109`, `OffsetEnd`** — `default: return 0` gives a new phase a **zero-width window**: `current_height >= formation_height + 0` is true on the first step, so the phase closes immediately and silently never runs. **This is ODC-050's class exactly** — a width-derived budget failing precisely when a width is the bug. **(4) `ptx_ceremony_driver.cpp:197` and `:240`** — a new phase emits nothing and never advances; downstream of (3) and surfaces as a stall rather than as wrong data. **(5) `ptx_sign_client.cpp:200` — `default:` is CORRECT here and removing it would be a DEFECT.** ★★ **THE RULE, which is the reusable output of this row, and it has two halves — quote both or it does damage:** ***`default:` is wrong on a closed, project-owned enum; it is right where the value is cast from untrusted input.*** The worked example is (5): the value is `(PTXSignStatus)resp.status`, cast from a byte off the wire sent by an **unauthenticated** peer, so any of 256 values can arrive and it is **not a closed enum at that point**. Stated as "no `default:` arms", the rule reads as a blanket instruction and someone will apply it to a wire-value switch and delete the only handling an attacker-supplied byte has. ★ **ACTED ON:** `PTXQuorumState` and `PTXDKGPhase` both gained `_COUNT` + `static_assert`, copying the KDD-085 shape — **zero runtime change, pure compile-time tripwire**, which is why they were safe to land pre-launch when the `default:` behaviours were not. `PTXQuorumStateName()` is now the one renderer of quorum state. ★ **DELIBERATELY NOT FIXED, both given triggers rather than left to age (the ODC-083 precedent — an acceptance without a trigger is a forgotten acceptance):** (a) `PTX_QuorumRecordActiveAt`'s `default: return false` — trigger is its own `static_assert`, whose message now names the function, states that it is consensus-visible, says the default silently excludes, and says mixed-code disagreement is a chain split, i.e. it instructs rather than mentions; (b) `OffsetEnd`'s `default: return 0` — **nothing pointed at this one**, so the trigger is now wired into `PTXDKGPhase`'s assert and the enum's own comment: *before the next phase is added*, which that assert will force. The tripwire that fires now tells the person what else to look at, which is the difference between a guard and a nuisance. ★ **STATED COVERAGE BOUND:** `PTXQuorumState` got a loop test over every enumerator (catching a MIDDLE insertion at test time, which the assert cannot); `PTXDKGPhase` did **not**, because `PhaseName` sits in an anonymous namespace (`ptx_ceremony_driver.cpp:13-131`) and is not linkable from tests. Widening a translation unit's API to test it was judged the wrong trade for a log-string helper; recorded here rather than silently skipped. ★ **The asymmetry that made any of this findable:** `PTXMemberSignState`'s guard proved itself only because a state was actually added and it fired. `PTXQuorumState` had a `default:` for as long as `REFORMED` existed and nothing ever announced it. ***A guard that has never been tested by an addition is indistinguishable from no guard*** — which applies to both tripwires added here, neither of which has yet fired on a real addition.

> **★ ODC-097 (raised 2026-09-02, QUESTION ONLY — NOT INVESTIGATED) — SHOULD CI BUILD WITH `-Werror` INCLUDING `-Wswitch`?** ODC-096 leaves nine of ten PTX enums resting on `-Wswitch`, which is a **warning**, and a warning in a 500-file build is read by nobody. `--enable-werror` already exists (`configure.ac:348`) and currently contributes only `-Werror=vla` and `-Werror=thread-safety-analysis`. Turning on `-Werror=switch` for CI would convert all nine from warnings nobody reads into build failures — **which is what everyone, including the reviewer who proposed the ODC-095 fix and the model who implemented it, assumed was already the case.** That shared wrong assumption is the argument for the row. ★ **What must be measured before deciding, and deliberately was not measured here:** how many warnings the tree currently emits under `-Wall` (the fork carries a large volume of upstream Bitcoin/Hemis code that nobody here has cleaned); how many of those are `-Wswitch` specifically; and therefore whether a `-Werror` CI leg is viable directly or needs a cleanup pass first, or wants a **narrow** `-Werror=switch` rather than blanket `-Werror`. ★ A narrow flag is the likely answer — it targets exactly the ODC-096 gap without adopting the whole upstream warning surface — but that is a guess and is recorded as one. ★ **Scope note:** captured with enough context to be picked up cold; **not** scheduled against launch, and explicitly not a blocker for it. Compile-time-only tripwires (ODC-096) already cover the two enums that mattered.

> **★★ ODC-098 (raised 2026-09-02) — FALSE GREEN FROM THE INSTRUMENT, NOT THE SYSTEM: THREE TIMES IN ONE SESSION, THREE DIFFERENT MECHANISMS, ALL OF THEM IN THE CHECKING TOOL RATHER THAN IN THE THING CHECKED.** ★★ **The rule: the check must be on the thing itself, not on something downstream of it.** A pipe, a wrapper, a formatter, a parser or a terminal escape sits between the fact and the assertion about it, and every one of them can silently drop or transform the signal being tested for. When that happens the result is not an error — it is a **pass**, which is the whole problem. ★ **The three instances, kept together because the pattern is only visible as a set:** **(1) A pipe ate the exit status.** `g++ ... 2>&1 | head -6; echo "exit=$?"` reports **`head`'s** status, which is 0 whatever the compiler did. Written while measuring *whether a missing enum case fails the build* — i.e. the instrument failed inside the very experiment about guards failing silently. **(2) A terminal escape defeated an anchored regex.** The px1 build script appends `EXIT=$?` to its log, but the preceding line's ANSI reset leaves an escape sequence immediately before the token, so `grep -E "^EXIT="` matched **nothing** and printed nothing. The log still said `*** No errors detected`, so the run *looked* green with **no status read at all**. Caught only by re-checking with `cat -A` and an unanchored `grep -a`. **(3) A tolerant parser validated an invalid file** — the ODC-092 arc: `yaml.safe_load` accepts duplicate mapping keys and returns a dict, so a workflow file that GitHub rejected with a 422 parsed clean locally. The check answered "valid YAML" to the question "will GitHub accept this". Replaced with an explicit duplicate-key detector. (A fourth, same family, from the same arc: **reading a diff cannot verify structure** — an edit silently swallowed a `- name:` key and the diff looked correct.) ★★ **The asymmetry that makes this a row rather than three notes:** in all three the *system under test behaved correctly* and its own guards worked. **The instruments reading them were weaker than the property they stood in for.** A session can therefore accumulate confidence with no correctness behind it, and nothing in the output distinguishes that from real confidence — a green from a broken instrument is indistinguishable from a green. This is the same shape as ODC-096's closing observation about untested guards, one level up: *the guard was fine; the thing watching the guard was not.* ★★ **AMENDED 2026-09-02 — THE COUNT IS THE FINDING, AND IT IS TEN, NOT THREE.** This row opened on three instances. By the end of the same session there were **ten**, every one in the checking tool rather than the system under test, and the system was correct in all ten. Enumerated so the recurrence cannot be read as anecdote: **(1)** `g++ ... | head` — `$?` reported `head`, not the compiler, *inside the experiment about guards failing silently*; **(2)** an ANSI reset before `EXIT=` defeated `grep -E "^EXIT="`, so a build looked green with **no status read at all**; **(3)** `yaml.safe_load` accepted duplicate keys GitHub rejected with a 422; **(4)** reading a diff to verify structure — an edit silently swallowed a `- name:` key; **(5)** `pgrep -f cold-sync-test.sh` matched **its own command line**, reporting a finished harness as RUNNING; **(6)** a staking waiter whose exit predicate was the literal `"stakeablecoins":10`, written when the nodes held 10 UTXOs and never updated when funding took them to 50 — it timed out while both nodes were staking correctly; **(7)** GitHub's API `size` rendered through `//1024` reported a 167-byte `SHA256SUMS` as **0 KiB**, i.e. "empty"; **(8)** `sha256sum -c` reported MISMATCH on **identical** hashes, because the checksum line names a relative path and the file had been saved under another name; **(9)** a Python `edit()` helper printed four `ok:` lines for replacements it then **discarded** — it aborted on a fifth pattern *before* the write, so the success messages described work that never reached disk; **(10)** `systemctl restart` returned 0 while the unit's `ExecStart` **exited status=1** — systemd accepted the job, the service failed, and the real daemon (a manual `Hemisd -daemon` holding the datadir lock) was never touched. ★★ **AMENDED AGAIN 2026-09-02 — THE COUNT IS FOURTEEN, AND TWO OF THE NEW ONES ARE A DIFFERENT CONSEQUENCE CLASS.** The first ten all misreported **to us**. These do not. **(11) A PUBLIC SURFACE ASSERTED A CAPABILITY IT HAD NOT CHECKED.** The verifier's footer said *"Txid lookup is enabled against <node>"* whenever `PTX_NODE_RPC` was **set** — configuration, not reachability. A reboot stopped the node and the sentence stayed. A visitor pasting a txid then got an error from a page that had just promised it would work — **on the one artefact whose entire subject is that claims can be checked rather than trusted.** ★ Same shape as the other thirteen, categorically worse consequence: the audience is a stranger who has no way to know the page is lying, and the subject is verifiability itself. **Fixed by probing (cached 15 s, 4 s timeout) and reporting three states — available / unavailable / not configured — with the node's height printed as evidence when it is up.** The unavailable text says the A/B/C checks still run, which is true and is the page's real claim. **(12) THREE TESTS WERE NEVER EXECUTED WHILE THE SUITE REPORTED A PASSING COUNT.** `test_decoder.py`'s `main()` named its tests **in an explicit list**, and `if __name__ == "__main__"` sat ABOVE three later definitions — so at the moment `main()` ran, those functions **did not yet exist in the module namespace**. The suite printed *17 passed* before and after they were added, and that number was read as covering them. It included the two committed specifically to pin the NOT-PERFORMED-vs-FAIL rendering. ★★ **A test that is never invoked is indistinguishable from a test that passes**, and the count moving is the only signal — which is why *"the number did not change"* deserves the same suspicion as a red result. Fixed structurally: the entry point moved below every definition, zero-arg tests are **discovered** rather than listed, and a leftover fails the run. Proven by adding a deliberately failing test: **20 passed, 1 failed, exit 1**; reverted, **20 passed, exit 0** — up from 17, which is the three that had never run. ★ **(13) and (14), both committed while investigating (12), and both worth recording rather than quietly fixing:** an orphan-detector that sliced the source from `def main(` and counted the later `def test_…(` lines **as calls**, reporting zero orphans when there were three; and a probe that **imported** the module — which executes the whole file — and therefore saw functions that do not exist during a real run. ★ The probe and the program disagreed because they were looking at different module states, and the probe was the one that looked right. **When an instrument contradicts observed behaviour, suspect the instrument first** — here the observed behaviour (the count not moving) was correct and two successive instruments explained it away. ★ **(15) 2026-09-02 — VERIFYING THAT AN ASSET IS SERVED RATHER THAN THAT IT RENDERS.** The branding report said the images were *"verified serving over HTTPS, and both pages reference them"*. Both statements were true and neither was the question. `curl` returned **HTTP 200, correct `content-type`, valid PNG** for every asset, and the pages did reference them — while the header lockup rendered its text below-and-right of the mark with the TESTNET wordmark missing entirely, and `/v2`'s icon showed as a blank box. **Referencing an asset and displaying it are different facts.** Both were found by a human looking at the page. ★ The fix was to stop asking the transport and start looking at the pixels: sampling each PNG into a text grid showed the lockup's geometry was wrong and that the "mark" was an amber frame around a white field — neither of which any HTTP-level check could ever have reported. ★★ **Generalisation for any visual output: the only instrument that answers "does this look right" is one that inspects the rendered pixels.** A 200 is evidence about a file, not about a page. ★ **The shapes worth naming, because they recur across languages and tools:** a pipeline reporting the wrong member's status (1, 10); a matcher defeated by formatting rather than by content (2, 7, 8); a parser more permissive than the consumer (3); a probe matching itself (5); a predicate that silently stopped describing its subject after the subject changed (6); and a success message emitted before the effect was committed (9). ★★ **The generalisation this amendment adds: an instrument decays exactly like documentation, and for the same reason — CHANGE, not age.** Instance (6)'s predicate was correct when written and false an hour later because the thing it measured changed; instance (9)'s helper was fine until a pattern failed. This is BUG-054's lesson applied to tooling, and it was demonstrated in the same session by a *documentation* line that went stale within two commits of being written by the same author who then had to correct it. **Drift is a function of change, not of age**, which is why a maintained list — of pin sites, of checks, of instruments — cannot be trusted and must be re-derived. ★ **THE PRACTICE, and it is the one that would have caught all three:** **prove the instrument against a known-negative before trusting its positive.** Feed it a case that MUST fail and confirm it says so. That is exactly the A1 discipline applied to tooling — the two enum tripwires were converted from "believed correct" to "observed to fail correctly" by deliberately breaking them, and a check on a build, a log or a config deserves the same treatment before its green is believed. ★ Concretely: read a process's own exit status rather than parsing its output; when output must be parsed, parse it once with a known-bad input and confirm the parser reports bad; and prefer `cat -A`/unanchored matching on any text that has passed through a terminal.

> **★★ BUG-055 (found 2026-09-02) — THE LAUNCH HOSTS' RPC IS BOUND TO A GLOBALLY-ROUTABLE IPv6 ADDRESS, CONTRADICTING THE `install.sh` IN THE TAG THEY WERE DEPLOYED FROM.** Found while running chain step 2's pre-mining gate on ptx001. ★ **The evidence, measured:** `install.sh` at the deployed tag (`c13a6ef` = `v0.2.0-testnet`) sets `RPCBIND_LINES="rpcbind=127.0.0.1\nrpcbind=::1"` — loopback only, which is the KDD-085 hardening. **ptx001's actual `Hemis.conf` carries FOUR `rpcbind` lines**: `127.0.0.1`, `::1`, `192.168.2.30`, and `2a07:244:46:6400::9100`. `ss -lntp` confirms the daemon is listening on all four, i.e. **an RPC listener on a public IPv6 address**. ★★ **SEVERITY, stated precisely rather than alarmingly: exposed, but REFUSING.** `rpcallowip` is loopback-only (`127.0.0.1`, `::1`), so a remote request is rejected at the allow-list **before authentication**. Measured from ptx002 against both the LAN v4 and the public IPv6 address: **HTTP 403** in each case. This is not an auth bypass, not a credential exposure, and not a data leak — it is an **unnecessary internet-facing listener** that answers strangers with a refusal. It is also the live confirmation of the 403-before-401 ordering captured as a RED leg during KDD-085 component 4. ★ **The cause is a STALE CONFIG, not stale code.** The conf was written from the pre-KDD-085 template — the one whose own comment says the per-global-address `rpcbind` list "existed solely so the [fan-out] could reach it". The fan-out is deleted; the reason for those lines died with it, but the deployed file predates the hardening. **The tag is correct and the host is not**, which is the failure mode a version check cannot see: `Hemisd -version` on ptx001 reports the right source. ★ **The generalisation worth keeping:** *deploying the right tag does not mean running the configuration that tag generates.* A config written once and carried forward outlives the code that justified it, and nothing in the binary, the tag, or the version string disagrees with it. ★ **`self-check.sh` §5's inverse check (RPC reachable externally = FAIL) is exactly the instrument for this and has not been run against these hosts** — it would have caught it, which is an argument for running it as part of bring-up rather than only on operator machines. ★ **NOT FIXED — deliberately.** The remedy is a conf edit plus a daemon restart on the three launch hosts; that is a change to running infrastructure and is Vileda's call, not a drive-by. It does **not** block the cold-sync harness: that harness is designed to run alongside its reference on the same host (`PTX_REF_RPC=127.0.0.1:...` in its own usage line), so loopback-only RPC is sufficient for it.

> **★★ ODC-099 (raised 2026-09-02) — THE PRODUCT IS A RANDOMNESS BEACON FOR GAME DEVELOPERS AND THERE IS NO MACHINE-READABLE WAY TO CONSUME IT.** The public explorer ships nine `/api` endpoints, all eIquidus's generic ones — blocks, difficulty, supply, txcount. **None is PTX-aware.** The only surface that understands a roll is `/v2`, a **human page with a paste box**: you hand it a txid or raw `extraPayload` hex and it renders decoded fields and checks A/B/C for a person to read. ★ **A developer integrating this cannot use any of it.** Fetching the beacon for a given height, listing rolls for a `game_id`, or verifying a result programmatically all require either scraping HTML or reimplementing the decoder against `getrawtransaction` — which is precisely the reimplementation `ptx_verify.py` exists to spare people, and the one most likely to be done wrong. ★★ **Why this is a real gap and not a nice-to-have:** *how does a game actually consume this* is the question the whole project exists to answer. Every other surface built so far answers *how do I operate a gamemaster* — a different audience with a different need. The verifier proves a roll is checkable **by a human who already knows what a roll is**; it does not let software depend on one. ★★ **VERIFICATION HALF BUILT AND CLOSED 2026-09-02; DISCOVERY HALF REMAINS OPEN WITH ITS TRIGGER INTACT.** Three JSON endpoints now live on the **Python** service beside `/v2` — not in eIquidus, because putting them there would mean reimplementing the decoder in a second language and keeping *two* things in sync with the C++ struct, which is precisely what `ptx_verify.py` exists to prevent: `POST /v2/api/v1/verify` (raw payload, no node), `GET /v2/api/v1/tx/<txid>`, `GET /v2/api/v1/commitment/<txid>`. ★★ **Every response carries `payload_hex`, and that is the anti-oracle guarantee** — decoded values without the bytes they came from would force a caller to *trust* the answer instead of re-deriving it, which is the privileged-answer surface `proxy.py` was retired for. Pinned by a test, not only by a comment, so a later "simplification" fails the suite. ★★ **THE COMMITMENT ENDPOINT FOUND THAT ITS OWN PREMISE WAS WRONG.** The plan said *orphaned past its settlement window*. **There is no consensus-enforced window:** `specialtx_validation.cpp:950` enforces only `nExpiryHeight >= nSeedHeight`, BUG-034 retired the same-block mandate, and the in-source comment is explicit — *"No upper bound is enforced: no candidate mechanism is consensus-real."* `nExpiryHeight` is declared **by the committer**. So the endpoint reports `settled` — a hard fact, since the settle SPENDS an output of the commitment (BUG-032 2c coin-chain), making an unspent output an O(1) `gettxout` proof that no settle exists — plus `past_declared_expiry` as arithmetic on a self-declared value, **labelled as such**. It never returns a verdict of *failed*, because the chain never reaches one. Asserting failure would have been the same overreach as `lastpaid` reading as *paid* when it means *selected*. ★ **Error model, three states rather than two, at both levels:** `404 not_found`; `400 malformed` (your bytes are broken); **`422 unsupported_payload`** — valid bytes of a struct this decoder does not model, found live when a type-5 LLMQ commitment was being called *malformed*, which tells an integrator their input is bad when it is not; and check D serialises as **`"ok": null`, never `false`**, since a JSON `false` is the machine-readable version of rendering NOT PERFORMED as FAIL. ★ **Rate limiting, measured before deciding:** verification is **0.03 ms/request, ~34,700 req/s single-threaded** — the checks are SHA re-derivations, not pairings (the BLS verify *is* check D, and it is not performed) — so `/verify` is unlimited. The two node-backed endpoints make **our** node do disk I/O for an unauthenticated stranger, which is BUG-052's caller-imposed-cost shape, so they carry 30 r/m per IP with burst 10 in nginx. Verified live: 20 rapid node-backed requests produced **9×429 / 11 served**, while 20 `/verify` requests produced **20×200**. ★★ **WHAT STAYS HONESTLY OPEN, and it is a real unknown rather than a schedule slip: nobody has told us what a game integration looks like.** Rolls-by-`game_id` assumes `game_id` is how a developer organises work; beacon-by-height assumes polling. They may want a webhook, a subscription, or something neither of us has imagined. **An API is a contract that cannot be quietly changed once someone integrates**, so building the wrong shape and having it depended on is worse than building nothing. The discovery half stays deferred until a real integrator's needs drive it. ★ **NOT A LAUNCH BLOCKER, and the trigger is explicit:** operators need the explorer, developers come later. This is scheduled at **the first request to integrate PTX from outside the operator set** — a game, a demo, or anyone asking for an endpoint rather than a page. Whichever comes first. ★ **What it would need, recorded now while the shape is fresh:** the decoder and the three checks already exist as a Python library (`ptx_verify.py`, `ptx_decode.py`) with the payload spec read from `SERIALIZE_METHODS` rather than hand-written — so a JSON endpoint is a wrapper over code that is already test-pinned by `test_decoder.py`, not new verification logic. ★★ **AND IT MUST BE ABLE TO SAY A ROLL FAILED — this is a CONSTRAINT on the design, not a feature request.** Worked through from source, there are three failure modes and **the chain records exactly one of them**: **(a)** funding failure throws `-32050` at `ptx_mempool.cpp:98` *before* the commitment is broadcast — nothing reaches the chain; **(b)** `no ACTIVE quorum for height N — cannot sign` is an RPC error to the caller — also invisible on chain; **(c)** `rpc/ptx.cpp:292` broadcasts the commitment **before** the fan-out, so a quorum that fails to reach threshold leaves a `PTXROLLCOMMIT` on chain **with no matching settle, and the fee forfeit**. ★ **So the on-chain signature of a failed roll is an orphaned commitment past its settlement window**, and that is the only failure an API can surface. ★★ **Why it is a constraint rather than a nice-to-have:** *"did my roll settle"* is the operational question for anyone integrating, and an endpoint returning only completed rolls answers the easy half. Worse, **a beacon that shows only its successes looks more reliable than it is** — the same category of misrepresentation as a `lastpaid` field that means *selected* while reading as *paid*, and as a page asserting a lookup capability it did not have. Whatever gets built must be able to say **"this commitment never settled"**. ★ **The constraint that must survive it:** any such API stays chain-derived and read-only, and must not become the authority — it has to return enough for a caller to re-derive the result themselves, or it recreates the privileged oracle the architecture was chosen to avoid.

> **★★ BUG-056 (found 2026-09-03) — `protx_register_fund` AND `ptxNodeId` ARE MUTUALLY UNSATISFIABLE, AND FOUR OPERATOR DOCUMENTS INSTRUCTED OPERATORS TO USE THEM TOGETHER.** Found while verifying the `/register` walkthrough's argument order against the released `v0.3.1-testnet` binary, after registration had failed for everyone who attempted it with `bad protx id suffix`. ★★ **The defect is circular by construction, not a typo.** The node id's suffix is `SHA256(serialize(collateral outpoint))[0:4]` (`rpcevo.cpp:478-487`). In the `_fund` path the collateral is created *by the registration transaction itself*, so `rpcevo.cpp:745` builds the outpoint as `COutPoint(tx.GetHash(), n)` — **read before `SignAndSendSpecialTx` writes the completed `node_id` into `extraPayload`.** Writing it there changes `tx.GetHash()`. The suffix committed on chain is therefore derived from a txid the final transaction does not have, and consensus recomputes it from the *resolved* outpoint and rejects the mismatch: `bad-protx-node-id-suffix`, `DoS(100)` (`specialtx_validation.cpp:170-179`). **There is no argument an operator could have supplied that would satisfy both ends.** ★ **The error names neither RPC**, so it reads as a malformed label — which is how it survived four rounds of guide correction aimed at the label rules. ★★ **`_fund` is not broken in general — it is broken exactly when `node_id` is non-empty** (`if (!pl.node_id.empty())`), which is the one configuration this chain requires: the pose tracker is a `std::map` keyed by node id, so unlabelled gamemasters **share a single penalty record**.
